Now: **eleven walkable in one connected grid** (Market Row / Uptown / Grand Plaza over Old Town / downtown / Midtown over the Lot / Trailhead / Outskirts, with a stem of Greenwood Park and Trout Lake running south off the Lot), plus Roadside, the Cave, the Terrace, the **Olympic Village** and the **farm** off the grid. Notown stays out, being a cut that has not been reversed. **Append-only, and it is not a style preference**: `EDirtbagZone` is a `uint8` and `DestinationZone` is an `EditAnywhere` property, so **every travel spot already placed in Evan's level stores its destination as a number** — inserting a zone anywhere but the end renumbers all of them at once, with no compiler error, no failing test and a symptom of *walking to the gym and arriving at a crag*. Lot..Terrace keep 0..4 forever, the grid lives in the adjacency table rather than the numbering, and the ordinals are pinned by a test. **Interiors are deliberately not zones** — the 2D game gives the cafe, diner, cabin and mall their own `ZoneId`s but everything hanging off that is camera work, and the sim already models a counter as a *spot*, which is the right grain. **The one tuned number survived, and that took arithmetic**: `lotToTownMinutes = 20.0` was tuned to be worth the van when the van runs and survivable when it does not, and **on the real grid the Lot and downtown are diagonal** — two borders either way round — so the dial is now `minutesPerCrossing = 10.0`, chosen precisely so that walk still costs its twenty minutes and every new walk is priced off it rather than off a guess. Widening a map must not quietly rebalance the route that was already right, and the only way to know it did not is to pin it. The longest walk is Trout Lake to Grand Plaza, six borders, an hour on foot — the quiet end to the expensive one. **Most of the new tests check my typing rather than a design**, because the adjacency table is hand-written and every link is entered twice, once from each end: reverse links (a link entered from one side only is a one-way street you can walk into and not out of), **connectivity** (an island reads at every call site as though it needed the van, because `WalkCrossings` answers -1 for both), and **distances pinned rather than ordered** — *"the lake is further than the Trailhead"* passes on a map where everything is one border from everything, which is exactly what a bad edit produces. **Verified by reintroduction, five defects**: renumbering fires 218 failures led by the ordinal pins, a one-way link 15, an island 8, the lazy `IsACrag` 3, a rebalanced border 2. **The first renumber attempt did not reproduce the defect and reported zero** — `Lot = 0` is explicitly assigned, so an enumerator inserted above it collides at 0 instead of shifting anything; a reintroduction that does not reintroduce is a test nobody has checked, the same trap as a checker that passes because it is not looking. **And the day a comment was written for arrived on schedule**: `IsACrag` was written longhand rather than as `NeedsTheVan`, with a note saying the checks *"cannot currently fail"* and were kept for the day comps turned up, because *"a comp is a van zone that is not rock"*. Comps came back this morning, the Village and the farm landed with them, and those checks are live guards now instead of a comment hoping somebody reads it. **The bug the widening created is the finding.** `WalkHours` worked out where you were walking *from* by looking at where you were walking *to* — *if the destination is town you must be at the Lot, otherwise you must be in town* — which with two zones is not a hack but a proof, and with eleven **is false without being an error**: the walk still costs a number and the number is somebody else's walk. Its own comment had promised the table would grow *"in one file and every spot pointing along it follows"*, which was true of the minutes and not of the origin. **The engine had no notion of which zone the player was in at all**, because with two zones you never needed one; `UDirtbagGameInstance::CurrentZone` is that notion, starting at the Lot because you wake in the van and set beside `SetVenue` on arrival, which is the one moment both *where in the world* and *what rock* change — after the hours are computed, since `WalkHours` reads it as the origin and would otherwise price the walk from where you are about to be. Not saved yet, recorded rather than done quietly: nothing in the save has ever held where you were standing, and it starts mattering when Phase 6 makes zones places you load back into. **This fix has no test and cannot have one from here** — engine-side, no Unreal compiler in the container — so what is checkable is checked and the rest is stated. Three new sim functions (`IsConnectedGround`, `Adjacent`, `NeighbourOf`) are marked `unwired-ok` rather than given Blueprint wrappers: `NeighbourOf` is the verb connected ground is built on and **there is no connected ground yet**, so wrapping them now would be three verbs with no door — this project's favourite bug, found at six layers already. They are not dead in the meantime, since `TestZones` walks every link from both ends through `NeighbourOf`, and preflight prints the unwired count on every run.

That is *2D minigames, 3D staging* exactly, and `COMP_PRESSURE = 0.8` (a comp keeps 80% of your normal margin, psych work buys back half) is one dial on a resolver that is already tested and has golden vectors. Also back: **the circuit season** (five comps on firm dates, the last a 1.5× finals, and no-showing hands the rival 60 points because a schedule is a commitment like the job); **three tiers that mean three different things**; **a field of people rather than a sorted list** — seven named climbers each with a discipline they are known for and one they are soft on, a *redistribution* rather than a buff, so Kai takes the dyno problem off you every time and you take the crimpy one back; **the national ranking** across boulder, sport and speed; **the national team as a roster and not a threshold** — five teammates on the paper, a head coach with opinions, a **$180 taxable stipend that does not cover rent**, a review that can take it all back, and **a climber you went past to get on it who knows**; **the World Cup** across ten real venues with **an international field that flies whether you do or not**, so the rounds you skip are counted against you; and **the Games** on a 56-day cycle where you arrive three days early to declare your disciplines and the problems sit a grade above yours. Leagues came with it as the same system's low end. **One balance failure is inherited pre-diagnosed and written into the gate**: the 2D field was originally symmetric around your grade, so four entrants sat above you before every comp *at every tier*, and it measured **5th on average, 2.2% podiums and 0% wins, forever** — gating three downstream systems behind an event that happened one comp in fifty. Local must be winnable. **The ladder sits at 9 rather than earlier because of a real dependency**: it is entangled with the rival throughout — season points, head-to-head record, scouting them before a comp, the points they bank on a no-show — and **comps without somebody to lose to are a leaderboard**, so rivals ship first. The old Phases 9–12 renumber to 10–13, and the standing cut count drops from fifteen systems to seven.

None of the ten existing checkers could see it — they check that declarations are *reachable*, and this is about whether the sim's two consumers **agree on which rules apply.** Checker eleven answers it, and **reports zero**: for the first time, the measured game and the played game are provably the same. **Getting it right took three tries and two of them passed while blind.** It saw **33 rules out of 92** — the declaration regex excluded `{` to avoid function bodies, which made it blind to every declaration with a braced default argument (`= DayDials{}`), which is most of this sim; it reported zero problems and all three divergences were in the part it could not see. **A checker that passes because it is not looking is worse than no checker.** Then it flagged `FactionDay` and `KitDay`, which are called inside `SleepToNextDay` and were working exactly as designed. Then counting any sim call as reach excused rules reachable only from `GoToTheGym`, which only the probe calls — a rule reached only from a dead function is not reached. - 2026-08-23 - **Physio gets a door, and the career that cannot afford it** (`OnPhysio`, `PhysioLine`, `notes/phase6-physio.md`). Fourth of the five no-door systems. §4 ports **injury + physio + aging** as one thing: injury is built and measured, aging is built, and **the recovery half had no way in** — you got hurt and you waited. That is the half that matters most, because it is the only purchase in this game that hands climbing *back*: every other one moves it around, and the dial comment says so outright. **`P` at the gear-shop counter** — its own key because E buys rubber — and **the counter is not a physio and the code says so**: there is no physio *place* because the town is not somewhere yet, the shop is already the town's one desk (rubber, pads, dreams, a sponsor's paperwork), and when the town becomes somewhere in its own right this earns its own door. The prompt states the trade in the only units that matter — *"$95, and about 6 days off it"*, days rather than "recovery" — and a refusal says which of the three reasons it is, as a physio rather than a locked door: *"The physio wants 4 more days before they see you again."* **Measured: physio roughly halves an injury**, at a stable ~$16–19 a day across every length from a week to four months. Strong, and meant to be — it is the answer to a gate open since Phase 3, where money has never once bought climbing. **But the finding that matters is not the price.** A full course for a thirty-day injury is **$285**, and the measured thirty-year `saver` career — the policy that actively tries to accumulate — peaks at **$447 cash ever held**, with ninety years across four generations peaking at $417 and a single year's high around $240. **So a full course is 64% of the most money that career ever held at once**, affordable roughly once, at the top of the best year a dirtbag ever has. Not necessarily wrong — *"I cannot afford the physio"* is the most authentic sentence in this game — but it means the system's strength was theoretical, and **nobody could have known while the door did not exist.** Two things have changed since $447 was measured and both cut the other way: dreams gave careers a reason to hold a balance rather than spend it, and the sponsor stipend became reachable an hour ago and is the first income here that does not cost climbing time. **Worth re-measuring at career scale** with a policy that sees a physio when it can — does a sponsored climber with a dream ever actually buy their season back? One smaller mismatch recorded: the dial comment prices physio *"against a week of bills, because you are buying a fortnight of your season back"*, but one $95 session buys six days and a fortnight back costs $285 across three weeks — the framing is per-session and the claim is per-injury.

Third of the five no-door systems, and the missing caller nobody would have guessed at: `SignWithSponsor` having no door was expected, but **`SponsorOwnsToday` had no caller either — and that is the whole system.** The sim header is unambiguous: *"A shift lands on any day. A photo shoot lands on a good one… Obligation days a month, and they land on days with a window. **This is the entire mechanic**: money that costs you the good days rather than the spare ones."* Nothing asked it, so even a reachable deal would have paid $640 a month and cost **nothing** — the one money in this game whose price is good days would have been the only free money in it. **Signing is at the counter on `S`**, its own key because E buys shoes and signing away three days a month should not be the same press as buying rubber; the shop mentions it only when the offer beats what you hold, and the offer always states **both halves** — *"a title sponsor - $640 a month, and 3 days a month that will not be the rainy ones"* — because an offer that says what it pays and not what it wants is an advert. **The obligation is taken at Sleep rather than offered as a choice**: you wake up and six hours already belong to somebody, which is what being sponsored is, and the decision was made when you signed. News is appended rather than assigned, since the month's money, the yearly review and a shoot can all land on one morning. **Measured over five years and eight seeds**: shoes 0 days, gear 6.0 good days a year for $1,440, title **18.0 good days for $7,680** — and **every day taken is a good day**, so the mechanic works exactly as written with no leakage onto rainy ones. Against a career earning about $7,461 a year from working, **a title deal roughly doubles your income and takes about 15% of your good days** — a real trade and a temptingly good one, which only became a trade at all once dreams gave money somewhere to go. **Two things found and deliberately not fixed**: `obligationsMetThisSeason` and `obligationsMissedThisSeason` are dead in three layers — unmirrored, unsaved, and **`ReviewSeason` resets them without ever reading them** — while the mirror-skip comment claims they are *"bookkeeping the review reads and then clears"*, which is wrong about the only thing it asserts, and is how the dead field survived. They are vestigial from a design where missing a shoot could cost you the deal; that is not the design the header describes, so making it real (two mirrored fields, a save bump, a new review arm and its tests) is recorded rather than done on my own initiative. **And the fifth ethical act is unblocked**: `StagedAPhoto` buys exactly one thing, the review's clock going back to zero — you did not send anything and they think you did — deliberately *not* a fake tick in the ledger, because the lie is told to the sponsor rather than to the book, which is why its Secret carries no route key and why `StripsTheAscent` finds nothing to take. **That exposed a bug in yesterday's work**: the wall had three shortcut keys and a comment claiming all four acts could never be offerable at once. They can, and it is the system's most interesting case — **a sponsored climber in a slump, standing under a line they have already fallen off** — who would have been shown a fourth option with no key to press. Four keys now, and the key indexes the *offered* list rather than the master list, so 2 always means the second thing the prompt actually showed.

Every number in `phase3-jobs.md`, `phase3-economy.md`, the dreams measurements and the hoarder sweeps was measured on a game that existed only in the harness. **The board itself is the argument for wiring it**: the flat shift was $15/hour, and the board runs from $11.67 for flyering to **$65/hour for shooting guidebook photos** — five and a half times the worst gig, two hours, almost no energy, and the one that turns the valley against you. That row *is* the system, and no player had ever been offered it. **The prompt is the board** — no opening ceremony, because a board is a thing on a wall you read — three numbered lines with pay, hours and energy, and `(needs the van)` said only when the van is off the road. **1/2/3 takes one and E deliberately does not**: the gigs are not interchangeable and a key that silently picked the best would hand somebody a standing hit they never chose, so E says where to look and the numbers do the work. A van-gated refusal is stated as a reason rather than a failure, because that is one more place a breakdown bites — it costs you the fix *and* the work that would have paid for it. **Two bugs fixed on the way.** Van guilt — the psyche cost of leaving a dog in a hot van — lived in `WorkShift` and not in `TakeOddJob`, so wiring the board as-is **would have quietly deleted the entire van-guilt system by making its one caller unreachable**; it moved. And `UDirtbagGameInstance::WorkShift` is now deleted, having become dead — **which `check-doors.py` missed**, because it counts `Name(` across the module and `UDirtbagSimLibrary::WorkShift` shares the name, so the instance's version looked called when its only caller was its own body calling the other one. Same blind spot `check-cues.py` had with `Van`, for the same reason: **a grep sees names, not scopes.** Found by hand, written into the checker's docstring, with the cheap fix named — do not give a verb a name the library also has.
- 2026-08-22 - **Ethics gets a door, and the benefit is the mirror of the punishment** (`TakeShortcut`, `CanTakeShortcut`, `notes/phase6-ethics-has-a-door.md`). First of the five no-door systems. **The discovery half was already complete and wired** — `SomebodyFindsOut` at Sleep, `ItComesOut` applying standing and psyche, `StripsTheAscent` clearing the send, `EthicsText` reaching the HUD — so what existed was *the consequences of an act you could not perform.* **T at the wall**, as a prompt state rather than a screen, because this is a thing you do in a moment at the bottom of a route and not a menu you open: *"Nobody is watching."* and what this line will take, 1/2/3 to do it, T again to think better of it, walking away withdraws it. **Offered without being urged** — a bare `(T)` on the end of the route line and never a sentence explaining what it would buy — and **nothing is congratulated**: one quiet grey line, because the comment arrives years later from everybody else. **The benefits were the real work**, since the verb records only that it happened, and each is built to be exactly what discovery takes back. Claiming and pulling-on both write `bSent` into the ledger, which is precisely the field the strip path already clears — perfect symmetry, no new machinery — and pulling-on requires you to have *been* on it, because one hang nobody saw is not the same lie as ticking a line you never tied into, which is why it costs 0.3 against claiming's 0.7. **Chipping was the interesting one**: its benefit is permanent and for everyone, and the obvious implementation is wrong, because **the crag is regenerated from the seed on every load** and a grade written onto a crag line evaporates at the next save. The secret persists, so the rock is derived from the secret — one clause in `GetRouteAt`, the single funnel every caller goes through. **Two acts deliberately not offered and recorded where somebody will read it**: retro-bolting needs runout plumbing that does not exist, and staging a photo buys a sponsor's goodwill when **there is no way to get a sponsor** — offering a shortcut whose benefit is a system you cannot reach would be this project's favourite bug wearing a new hat. **Measured, because the door creates a balance question the system did not have**: thirty years, 24 seeds. A nobody who claims forty lines **keeps twenty-three**; a star who claims forty **keeps two**. Success is what exposes you, exactly as designed, and it is self-correcting — the ticks you keep raise your grade record, which raises visibility, which is what catches you. Two findings recorded rather than fixed: **standing loss saturates at ~1.86**, so twenty claims and forty cost the same reputation and at scale the deterrent is the stripping rather than the shame (defensible — once you are a known cheat, being a bigger one costs nothing socially); and **the header slightly oversells the quiet career**, promising *"rather better than even odds of it never coming out"* where a single secret at zero visibility measures **40%**, not better than half. And `ThingsNobodyKnows` got its door too — the HUD's slow numbers, silent at zero, because a counter reading "0 secrets" every day of a clean career is a counter suggesting you get some.
- 2026-08-22 - **Five more systems with no door, and the tenth checker** (`tools/check-doors.py`, `notes/no-doors.md`). Evan: *"what else is written and never wired"* — so I hunted instead of guessing, by taking every Blueprint-exposed verb on `UDirtbagGameInstance` and counting real call sites, telling a definition from a call by whether a `{` follows the parameter list, which is the only way that works without a parser. **Fifteen came back and eight are real.** The **odd-jobs board** (`TodaysJobBoard`, `TakeOddJob`) — §4 port-wholesale, and the Shift spot is a generic *"Take a shift?"* that never touches it, so choosing *which* work to take, the interesting half, is unreachable. **Physio** (`SeeAPhysio`) — §4 ports *injury + physio + aging* as one thing; injury is built and measured and aging is built, and **the recovery half cannot be visited**: you get hurt and you wait. **Gym membership** (`RenewGymMembership`) — nothing renews it, and it gates the board and the kit. **Sponsorship** (`SignWithSponsor`, `SponsorOwnsToday`) — Phase 4 scope, built, measured, saved and migrated; the HUD would happily print a deal you cannot sign, and nothing ever asks whose day it is. **Ethics** (`DoSomethingYouWouldNotAdmitTo`, `ThingsNobodyKnows`) — §4 port-wholesale; `EthicsNews` reaches the HUD so the consequences would show, but **the verb that causes them cannot be pressed.** Seven more were legitimate and are annotated as such: five Blueprint accessors, and the two naming verbs the one widget calls — with a **desk item attached**, because the only `CreateWidget` in the module is Epic's mobile-controls one, so if nothing in Blueprint creates `WBP_NameFirstAscent` then naming a first ascent has no door either, and the HUD line added as a backstop is a backstop rather than a door. **The checker** requires a C++ call site or a reason — `// blueprint-only:` or `// no-door: <system>` — with scope deliberately limited to the game instance, because `DirtbagSimLibrary` exists to expose the sim to Blueprint and an unused entry there is dead weight rather than a missing door; flagging it would be crying wolf, which is the one thing a checker must never do. (Under the same analysis the library has 13 unused entries, mostly a parallel live-attempt wrapper set the wall bypasses by calling `dirtbag::` directly.) **`no-door` counts print on every preflight run**, so the eight stay visible rather than quietly becoming permanent — the difference between recorded debt and a silent hole, and this project has now shipped six silent holes and found every one of them by hand, late, and by accident.
- 2026-08-22 - **The guidebook, and a live function answering from constants** (`FDirtbagGuidebookReadout`, `DrawGuidebook`, `PeakGradeEver`, `notes/phase6-the-guidebook.md`). `Sim/DirtbagCrag.h` carried three `unwired-ok` notes all saying *"the guidebook page's filter; **there is no guidebook screen**"* — `LinesUpTo` and `OpenProjects` written for a page that did not exist. Without it you learn about a line by **walking up to it**: no way to see what is at a crag before touring it, which lines are done, where the open projects are, or how many burns are in the thing that keeps spitting you off. That last is the worst of it — §4 lists **projecting/nemesis tracking** as port-wholesale, `ProjectMemory` has tracked attempts, highpoint, beta and cleanliness *per line* since Phase 1, and it has only ever surfaced as **"attempt 14"** on the wall you happened to be standing at. The tracking was ported; the seeing was not, in a game whose whole loop is *pick a line, try it* and whose fiction is a guidebook. **G at any spot or wall**, three pages on 1/2/3 — everything, projects (wires `OpenProjects`), in reach (wires `LinesUpTo`) — each line read as the book would read it with **your pencil marks in the right-hand column**, and a header saying how much of the crag you have got through, which the game has never said anywhere. Colour does one job: done is quiet, unclimbed is gold, the rest is plain. **Nothing is silently swallowed** — a canvas has no scrollbar, so it caps and says *"7 more below the fold"*, and a filter says *"12 not on this page."* **Two hazards, both real.** `EnsureCrag` falls through to Roadside at the Gym, so pressing G indoors would have handed you the page for a crag forty minutes away; it refuses now and **says why** — *"Plastic. The setter's tag is the whole of the book here"* — detected by asked-to-open-and-still-shut rather than by re-testing the venue at the call site, so the rule lives in one place. And **G would have double-fired**: every spot and every wall binds it and the Lot has four spots within a few metres, so standing between the van and the fire would open and instantly close the book, which looks exactly like a broken key. **But the audit's real find was underneath.** The *in reach* page needs `PeakGradeEver` — read by `TimeToThinkAboutIt`, reset by `RetireAndPassItOn`, and **written by nothing.** A permanent `0.0`, which makes the *"two grades off your best"* arm of that function dead code; and its other arm reads `ConsecutiveInjuries`, **also never written.** So **`TimeToThinkAboutIt` has never been able to return true, for either reason, in the life of this project** — including for the handover shipped an hour earlier, which is triggered by it. Written-and-never-wired at a **sixth** layer and the nastiest kind yet: the others were dead features, a system with no verb and a phase with no door, while this is a **live function silently answering from constants**, invisible to every checker here because they prove declarations are reachable rather than that fields are ever assigned. Both are written at Sleep now — `PeakGradeEver` as a high-water mark of ability grade, stored rather than derived because you cannot recompute the best you ever were from the body that is left, and `ConsecutiveInjuries` counted on the day an injury *starts* and reset by a clean season rather than a clean day, because three injuries in a row is the signal and one injury lasting three months is not.
- 2026-08-22 - **You could not end a career** (`FDirtbagHandoverReadout`, `ThreeWhoCouldTurnUp`, `NameTheClimber`, `notes/phase6-the-handover.md`). Asked what else could be built without the editor, audited instead of guessing, and found something worse than a gap: **`RetireAndPassItOn`, `TimeToThinkAboutIt`, `CareerEpitaph`, `GenerationsBefore` and `InheritedGuidebook` were all BlueprintCallable and nothing called any of them.** No C++ caller, no Blueprint. Phase 4's headline system — legacy and generations, measured across ninety years and four generations, with inheritance rules, a save migration and crew names carrying over — worked perfectly and **could not be performed**. Written-and-never-wired at a fifth layer and the largest instance yet: the others were a system without a verb, this is a whole phase without a door, and the reachability checker cannot see it because it proves *sim* declarations reach the engine rather than that engine functions reach play. **A second hole fell out on the way in**: there was no way to name a climber *at all* — `ClimberName` is an EditAnywhere string with no in-game setter — so **every first ascent in the game was signed "you"**, and the crew hash, given the player's name three days ago *specifically* so four generations would stop being one generation four times, **was being fed an empty string.** That fix was measured, logged, and had never once worked in play. **R at the van**, on its own key because E repairs it and a career should not end because somebody meant to fix a wheel bearing, and with its own confirm because it is the most irreversible thing in the game and the save is written immediately; walking away disarms it. The prompt is **silent about stopping** until `TimeToThinkAboutIt` has something honest to say — never a command, never age alone — because a line offering retirement every night of a career is a line you learn to stop reading. Three beats: the epitaph, hand-wrapped because the canvas draws a string as one line however long it is and twenty years running off the screen edge is a poor way to end them; **somebody turns up**, three names on 1/2/3; then who you are now and **IN THE BOOK**. **Naming is a pick rather than a text box** — typing needs a widget, an editor and a focus fight, picking needs three keys the player already uses for the dream and the stake, and it reads better: you are not naming yourself, you are meeting the person who rolled into the Lot. The same screen names a fresh unnamed climber, with no confirm, because putting a name to yourself is not irreversible the way stopping is. **Three bugs caught, two by tests.** The name pool had **Dev and Bo** in it, both Lot regulars — the handover would have introduced you to somebody standing behind it; the check stays in the test rather than becoming a filter, because a filter silently absorbs a name added to either list later while a test fails and says which one. **The book was backwards**: the first version drew `InheritedGuidebook()` under the epitaph, but that walks the *filed* careers, so it listed your **predecessors'** lines under your own obituary — it is read at the arrival now, where it means what its name says and is the better beat besides. And the van prompt **would have rendered as a box**, having gained a second line when the canvas draws a string as one line however many newlines are in it; `PushPrompt` splits into separate prompt lines now. **Deliberately not built:** choosing between three whole *successors* rather than three names — `Inherit` already rolls skills and morphology, so offering three different bodies the resolver reads differently is a small change and a better moment, but it is a design decision and this was a wiring job. **This unblocks Phase 4's gate**, which asks that a career reads like somebody lived there — it needs a career ending in a handover, and the only evidence was headless because the handover could not be performed at the desk.
- 2026-08-22 - **The road exists** (`FDirtbagTravelReadout`, `DrawTravel`, `notes/phase6-the-travel-screen.md`). The 2D game shows the van crossing a background on its way somewhere; the port had fade, teleport, fade. **A fade says *time passed* and nothing else** — and this is **the only time the van is ever on screen as a vehicle** rather than as a thing you sleep in and repair, while being the thing every system in the game points at: the buffer a dream costs, the thing that breaks and takes a week off you, the reason a bad night at the fire is felt twice. The player had never once watched it go anywhere. It earns its keep twice over by being the one place a **walk** can look different from a drive — no van, no fuel line — which is the distinction the zone model was built to make and which otherwise lives entirely in a prompt. Shows where you are going, the van entering off one edge and leaving off the other (it never starts or stops in frame, because a van parked at the screen edge for a beat reads as a bug), **the clock running** because a forty-minute approach eats a window and a number that ticks says so better than one that jumps, the fuel, and **the breakdown said on the road** — where it happened, and which the game has never been able to show. It takes the whole screen and everything else stops drawing: you are between places, there is no decision available, and a pump bar over the top would be furniture. **Any key skips it** — the first trip is a moment and the fiftieth is a keypress, and a career has hundreds. **The ordering is the load-bearing decision**: the sim's whole side of the trip — hours, fuel, wear, the breakdown roll, the venue — is applied **once, up front, before a frame is drawn**, and the screen then applies nothing. Quit halfway down the road and the world is already in the state of having arrived, which is consistent; applying at the end would leave a half-taken trip on the floor. The displayed clock is therefore interpolation only, **wrapped rather than clamped**, because a drive leaving at 23:30 and taking forty minutes arrives at 00:10 and a clamp would sit at 23:59 claiming the trip took half an hour longer than it did. Backdrop and van are optional per-spot slots — per-spot because the road to the Cave should not look like the road into town — and with nothing assigned it draws a sky, a ground, a horizon and a shape. **And `check-cues.py` broke three ways in a row under it, each found by reintroduction rather than reasoning**: it searched only a header's paired `.cpp`, which is a false positive on any readout struct, since those are declared on the game instance, filled by a spot and drawn by the HUD — the entire pattern this presentation layer uses; the field was called `Van` in a module that already has `Van`, `VanRuns`, `VanLine` and `VanNews`, so a word-boundary search called it used no matter what; and it counted `T.Backdrop = RoadBackdrop` as a use, proving only *somebody fills this*, which is exactly the half that is never the bug. **Then it stopped improving**: after all three fixes, deleting the draw call still passes, because `if (T.Backdrop)` is a null-guard and a null-guard is a genuine read. Telling *guarded* from *drawn* needs a parser and this is a grep, so **the limit is written into the checker's own docstring** instead of left to be discovered — it still reliably catches the case it claims, a slot nothing in the module mentions at all, and `TopOutSound` and `FallAnim` both fire on it. A checker that says what it cannot do is worth more than one that implies it does everything.
- 2026-08-22 - **The port had one travel rule where the 2D game has two** (`Sim/DirtbagZones.*`, `DestinationZone`, `notes/phase6-the-place.md` corrected). Evan, on the Phase 6 plan: *"there are supposed to be attached zones for each location. the original dirtbag game… had zones connected except for crags and the olympics which you needed to use the van to get to. and then when traveling you saw the van moving across a background."* CLAUDE.md is explicit that **the 2D game is the spec**, and the port had departed from it with no decision recorded — so this is a correction, not a feature. **The plan I wrote yesterday was half wrong and the wrong half was load-bearing**: it said the six locations never need to be geographically plausible relative to each other because travel teleports. True of crags; false of the Lot and the town, which are **connected ground you walk between and which therefore have to look like one continuous place.** Two tiers: walk to the Lot and town, drive to Roadside, the Cave, the Terrace, and comps when they exist. **The correction exposed a live bug worth more than the model.** Every travel spot in the build was gated on the van running, so a breakdown cost you the crags *and the gym, the gear shop, the diner and the shift* — the van breaking took the whole game away instead of taking the rock away. **That is not pressure, it is a pause**, where the 2D game's version is a week of being stuck in town earning the money for the part, which is exactly the week the economy was built to produce. `Zone` is deliberately not `Venue`: a venue is *what rock* and knows only Gym/Crag/Cave/Terrace, while a zone is *where in the world* and the Town zone holds the Gym plus three destinations that are not climbing at all — collapsing them is what puts the diner on the guidebook. Travel spots carry a `DestinationZone` **defaulting to Roadside, a crag, so every spot already placed behaves exactly as it does today** until deliberately told otherwise; a default that silently made drives free is the worse direction to be wrong in. A walk costs time and *nothing else* — no fuel, no wear, no breakdown roll — and the prompt says "Walk to town? (E) — 20 minutes, no van needed", because "no fuel" is the reason you would choose it on a week when the van is sick. **One test honestly downgraded**: the checks that `IsACrag` names its members rather than being written as "not the Lot and not the town" **cannot currently fail** — with five zones the two definitions are equivalent, and reintroducing the lazy one passes the whole suite. Recorded as intent rather than dressed up as a guard, because it starts discriminating the day comps arrive, a van zone that is not rock. **Still not built and named as next: the travel screen.** Today it is fade, teleport, fade. The 2D game shows the van crossing a background, and that is not decoration — it is the only time the van is on screen as a *vehicle* rather than as a thing you sleep in and repair, and the van is what the money is for. A fade says time passed; a van crossing a background says this is what you keep alive. **And on one big city:** recommended against for now, on the grounds that the game has four things to do in town and a city of two hundred buildings with four doors that open reads as a film set — every street you cannot enter is a promise the game breaks. Lay the block out as a *corner* of a town rather than an island, and growing it later is generating more of what is already there, which is what the generator is for.
- 2026-08-22 - **Phase 5 closes, Phase 6 opens: the game has no world** (`concepts/DECISION-early-access.md`, `concepts/DECISION-art-direction.md`, `notes/phase6-the-place.md`). Items 1–4 made the session legible and item 5 made the shipping call — **Early Access is the plan and it is far off** — but the sentence that came with the call outranks all five: *"we dont even have anything built. just testing spots on the playground template… i dont even know what assets to use or how to put things together."* Twenty-six sim modules, a ninety-year headless career and nine preflight checkers, played on Epic's third-person template with trigger volumes standing in for a valley. **The container built the part it could verify, which was never the part at risk** — §9 named the top risk as *"session isn't tense/legible in 3D"* two years ago and §7 named the craft centre as the staging, and Phase 5 was the first work aimed at either. **Two of §11's four open questions answered** on the way, because assets cannot be chosen without them: **stylized characters on realistic rock** (the doc's own recommendation, and right for a reason beyond cost — a realistic human playing back six authored loops on a spline looks broken and a stylized one looks fine, and this project decided months ago it would never simulate climbing) and **buy anything that saves a build week**. Third person is written down as decided-by-construction rather than left listed as open. **The audit turned up two corrections to the shopping list itself, both made in place.** §7's *largest* entry — three climbing/IK plugins — is **obsolete by a decision already taken**: *"no physical climbing simulation, no hand-IK"* means none of them are needed, and the `ClimbingAnimationSet` already installed likely fills all six of the wall's animation slots, so the biggest available saving costs nothing. And §7's *"Megascans — free for UE"* is **stale**: free Megascans ended 31 December 2024, the library moved to Fab and is priced individually now. Still the right rock, no longer a freebie. **The plan's load-bearing finding is structural rather than commercial**: `ArriveFromDrive` calls `Pawn->TeleportTo`, so travel is a fade and a teleport inside **one level** — no sublevels, no streaming, no map-per-crag, already decided by code nobody had read for this purpose. And because it teleports, **the six locations never need to be geographically plausible relative to each other; they need only to not be visible from one another.** The valley does not have to be a valley. It is six pockets, and a solo dev can build six pockets. Ordered by use rather than by taste: the Lot first, because you sleep there every night of a thirty-year career and it is the most-seen place in the game by an order of magnitude; the Cave and the Terrace last, because they are seasonal and can stay grey for months. Within each: blockout, walkable, dress — never dress first. The climb walls wait until last and the rock purchase blocks nothing, because a wall is a spline and the rock behind it is scenery. One hazard flagged rather than discovered: **hand-drawn hold splines against photoscanned rock**, where a hold floating six inches off the surface reads instantly as wrong — do one wall and find out the cost before committing to twenty-five.

A cue fired flat is a sound effect; a cue whose pitch and level come off the numbers the camera reads is staging. **Chalk is gated and that is the point**: `ChalkBelowOdds = 0.7`, read off *the same field the camera tightens on*, so the shot coming in and the chalk going on say the same thing about the next move, one to the eye and one to the ear — nobody chalks for a jug, and the silence between cues is what makes one mean something. **Breath is the one that matters**: a loop on an audio component attached to the climber so it goes up the route with them, volume and pitch driven by pump, fading over 0.6s rather than cutting because an attempt ends with somebody getting their breath back and not with the air being switched off. The camera reads it now instead of its own square, so the eye and the ear cannot drift apart. **A test that passed on a bug, and the reason is worth keeping**: reintroducing a linear curve failed two checks, but deleting the quiet zone entirely *passed the whole suite* — because the checks were written as `PumpShows(quietBelow) == 0` and `PumpShows(quietBelow - 10) == 0`, both trivially true when the dial is zero. **A check phrased in terms of the dial it is checking cannot fail when that dial is wrong.** Pinned at absolute pump values now, and it fires. **Determinism in the one place nobody would have looked**: the move cue's pitch varies off a hash of the hold index rather than `FMath::Rand`, so a replay sounds identical to the attempt it replays — the same no-reroll discipline every gamble here lives under, applied where nobody would ever have noticed it was broken. **And checker nine**, because an EditAnywhere sound slot that nothing plays is **the quietest possible bug**: the symptom is that nothing happens, which is also exactly what an unassigned slot looks like, indistinguishable from inside the editor and invisible to any compiler. `check-cues.py` requires every `USoundBase` and `UAnimSequence` slot to be named somewhere besides its own declaration — written-and-never-wired one layer out from `check-unwired.py` — verified by reintroduction on both types, and it audited the six pre-existing animation slots on the way past, all wired. **Deliberately not built:** ambient loops. And they are **not one game three times** — poker asks you to read the person and rapport *is* the skill; liar's dice asks whether he is lying and whether you dare say so; **blackjack asks nothing social at all**, which is exactly why it earns its place, being the game for a climber who has just arrived and knows nobody, a real state here that nothing else pays off. **Liar's dice**, measured over 40,000 rounds × 4 seeds: passing costs the ante, calling everything loses **$22.56 a round** — punished harder than anything in the three — reading a stranger loses $7.69, which is right because you cannot tell if somebody is lying when you met them Tuesday, and reading somebody you know wins **$6.88**. **Blackjack**: played well it costs **$5.07** against a $5 ante — the ante and nothing more, the game with no edge to build rather than the game that punishes you for sitting down. **Three more magnitude bugs, all caught by measuring and none catchable by orderings.** `bidAmbition` began at 1.35, putting the bid well above what the dice held, so nearly every bid was a lie and **calling blindly won $19.21 a round** while playing the tell was *worse* than reflex. The tell was a **perfect lie detector**: poker's blur `truth*q + noise*(1-q)` applied to a *binary* question stops overlapping at any `q >= 0.5`, so rapport 0.5 and 1.0 scored **identically to the cent** — symmetric noise around a signal now, so a liar reads 0.45–1.05 against an honest 0.05–0.55 and the sliver between is the friend of ten years still getting you. And blackjack at 1.2 pay with ties to the player was **profitable at every strategy anybody would use**, which is not a game, it is an ATM; ties push now. All three fail on reintroduction (3, 1 and 1 checks). **One near-miss recorded**: reintroducing the blur first appeared to fail nothing, because it left a variable unused, `-Werror` rejected the build, and grepping only for `FAIL` read an empty result as a pass. **A reintroduction that does not compile is not a passing test.**

First was the instrument, not the game: every run reported **0 aggravations**, including one with **274 days climbed on while injured**. `ClimbOnIt` fires per burn during the session, and the probe snapshotted `injury.severity` immediately before `SleepToNextDay` — *after* the day's climbing — so the counter was sampling after the event it was trying to detect and could only ever read zero. Snapshot moved to dawn, comparison moved to before sleep because sleep is what heals: **0 aggravations became 115**. Second, and a real design question: **injuries are gated entirely behind kit.** greedy, projector, stakeout, salary and sponsored take **zero injuries across ninety years and four lifetimes**; `saver` takes 13 and `kitted` 22, and the only thing separating them is a board and a gym membership. Not a tendency, a switch. The mechanism is load and it is coherent — `stakeout` climbs 1,994 outdoor days for 5,652 burns, `kitted` climbs **fewer** outdoor days for **11,304** burns because 2,743 gym days and 7,989 board sessions happen when the weather refuses, so average load is 0-1 without kit and 38-41 with it. Outdoor climbing is rationed so hard by weather, skin and work that **load never stacks faster than it sheds**. That is either the best thing in the phase — the dirtbag who only climbs on good rock never gets hurt because the weather is their training plan, and you have to *buy* your way into enough volume to wreck your fingers — or a hole in it, because Phase 4 names injuries, physio and aggravation as scope and a player who never buys a membership meets none of them in a twenty-three-year career. **No dial changed**: which reading is right is a design call, not a measurement. The note argues both and names the honest lever if outdoor-only should be able to hurt you (load shedding, not injury chance). Also retired two stale bullets: the belayer is fully wired and crag 3 is built.

A `Legacy` already carries the key, the name, the confirmed grade and who they were, so the new overload writes straight from it, and the engine applies legacies before the living player's ledger on every crag load. After: **2 named lines across 4 lives** instead of the same rock four times. **And one thing left open on purpose**: generations 2–4 put up *no* first ascents, because Roadside has five projects, generation one takes the V3 and the V5, and the rest are V7–V9 and filthy. The ninety-year guidebook reads like **one person lived there and three more failed**. Also fixed on the way: a probe bug that carried `player.day` across inheritance and **birthed each climber at the age their predecessor retired**, which made the first run look like a catastrophic aging collapse; and an unsigned underflow that reported 18,446,744,073,709,551,614 open lines remaining.

- 2026-08-21 - **Crag 3: the Sun Terrace, and what aspect actually does** (`SunTerrace`, `EDirtbagVenue::Terrace`, `notes/phase4-crag3.md`). Measured the four aspects across a year before authoring anything, and the measurement said something better than the story would have. **In high summer North, South and West are identical** — not similar, identical: 25 days, 0.880 friction, a 1.34h window at 5:30am — because the summer window lands *before the sun is on any face at all*. **That refutes the Cave's stated reason for existing**, and its own next clause is why: *"in high summer, when the season model puts the window at dawn and nowhere else, this is the only rock worth walking to"* — the window being at dawn is exactly what makes north-facing worth nothing there. Written from climbing folklore, true of real crags, never checked against this model; `notes/phase4-cave.md` now carries the correction. The Cave is still the only rope crag and still costs a belayer, which is plenty. **Aspect earns its keep in winter**: south gets **42 days against north's 28**, in a window less than half as long (1.30h against 3.06h) landing at about **1:45pm**. So the Sun Terrace is south-facing boulders, high and cold — eleven lines V3–V11 and two projects at V10 and V12, deliberately **sparser and harder than Roadside**, because it is not somewhere you go instead of Roadside but somewhere you go in January or once Roadside has stopped being hard enough. **And one claim I nearly shipped and measured down:** "the crag you cannot climb around a job" is false — a salaried climber reaches **22 of the terrace's 63 winter windows against 13 of the cave's 48**, more in both count and proportion. What is true is narrower: **the terrace is where a job costs you the most**, 41 good days lost against the cave's 35, because it has the most winter days to lose and they land in the middle of them. Leaves open that aspect is a weak lever for three quarters of the year and `approachHours` is still read by nothing — between them, most of what would make one crag feel unlike another.

The sim side was already well tested, so the new test covers only the property the wiring depends on and nothing pinned: **`BestBelayer` returns the most patient willing partner, not the first**, and not the one you know best if they cannot climb. - 2026-08-21 - **The pad is a trade now, and the shop says so** (`mostFoamCanDo`, `PadOfferText`). Both recommendations from the gate restatement, measured rather than guessed. **The cap:** at `padsThatMatter = 2` a second pad put padding at exactly 1.0, exposure at exactly 0 and head training at **+0.00 in eleven of twelve seasons**, permanently, since head never declines either — a switch, not a decision. Swept across twelve seasons of a player who buys the pad: head over a year runs 1.00 → **+0.01**, 0.90 → +0.41, **0.85 → +0.58**, 0.80 → +0.78, 0.75 → +0.99, while the send median holds at **5.0 all the way down to 0.85 and then falls off a cliff to 3.5**. So 0.85 is the knee: it restores a third of the head a one-pad season trains (+0.58 against +1.78) and **keeps every send**. Below it you would be buying head by making the pad not worth owning, which is the same switch pointing the other way. It is also just true — a well-padded highball is still a highball; you can cover the landing and you cannot make the ground stop being down there. Shipped build, 12 seeds: saver **median 5.0 / trimmed 4.2 sends, head +0.58, 169 days, 20.9% worked** against greedy's **4.5 / 3.2, +1.85, 181, 19.5%** — winning 6, tying 1, losing 5, which for a *decision* is the right shape: a purchase that always wins is not one either. **The legibility:** the gear shop now offers the pad in the same breath as its price — *"A second pad, $260. Better landings, and one less reason to be brave."* — and it was previously reachable **only from a Blueprint call**, so the item measured to move a season most could not be bought at any shop in the game. Threading that through also fixed a smaller thing the dial checker's cousin would have caught: `StartGymSession` built its own `KitDials{}` internally, so what the pads under you are worth was not reachable from any caller. **All three gate criteria now pass on measurement and none has been played**; the marker is not advanced here, because a legibility criterion is not met until a person reads the line and knows what it means.
- 2026-08-21 - **Criterion 2 passes, and the correction is the more useful half.** Within the hour of writing that the second crash pad was unreachable — *"$235 and never reaches its $260"* — the reason turned out not to be the economy. **Every policy in the probe works only when nearly broke** (`cash < 120`), which is a thermostat, and a thermostat never saves up. The pad was not unreachable; **no simulated player had ever tried to buy it.** Exactly the shape the first Phase 3 note named and did not finish chasing — *"a thermostat cannot tell you how cold it is outside"* — except that was written about the income side, and the **spending** side had it too and nobody looked. A `saver` policy that works on any day it is short of the pad buys it inside a season for **six extra working days a year** (21.1% against 19.5%) and **thirteen climbing days** (168 against 181), returning about a send: median **5.0 against 4.5**, trimmed mean **4.3 against 3.2**, winning 7 seeds, tying 2, losing 3. *(The untrimmed mean of 5.8 against 3.5 is inflated by one seed returning 23; the median is quoted first on purpose.)* A decision with a price, which is what the criterion asks. **But the same run found a design problem the gate does not cover:** the pad's price in head is not a cost, it is an **erasure** — `padsThatMatter = 2` puts padding at exactly 1.0, exposure at exactly 0, and head training at **+0.00 in eleven of twelve seeds, permanently**, since head never declines either. The trade reads *"about one send a year in exchange for every point of head you will ever have"*, which is a switch rather than a decision — nobody weighing it would find it interesting, they would find out afterwards. The cave is the designed escape hatch and is not placed yet. **Capping padding below 1.0**, so even a well-padded highball keeps a trickle of exposure, would make it proportionate; that is a design call about whether foam ever fully removes fear, and it is not made here.
- 2026-08-21 - **Phase 3's gate, restated** (`notes/phase3-restating-the-gate.md`, ROADMAP). The original — *"the money loop pressures the climbing loop the way the 2D game's does"* — is retired after **six measurements failed it**: bills, fuel, the kit, physio, sponsorship, and finally deleting the cost of being alive outright on a control that could afford shoes. It could never have been met. **A season's burn count is set by skin supply, then injury, then the weather, and money touches none of them** — 208 days a year have a window, a player climbs about 180, total burns sit near 520 and barely move, and seventy days of work fit entirely inside the days that were never climbable anyway. That is why work is free and why buying the days back buys nothing. Asking a money system to move a number money does not touch is not a hard gate, it is an **unmeetable** one, and it yields a failed measurement and no information about the mechanic. What money *does* do is measured and consistent: **shoes** (never replacing them takes a season from 3.7 sends to 1.0 on the same burns, 7 wins 3 ties 0 losses, for about $9) and **the second crash pad** (+37%, and it spends no skin) — both *kit that raises the value of skin you were going to spend anyway*, the category `notes/phase3-kit.md` identified before it had a gate to belong to. **Money buys quality, not quantity: you cannot buy more burns, you can buy burns that count.** So the gate is now **"money decides how good your burns are, and deciding is something the player actually does"**, in three measurable parts: neglecting kit visibly costs you climbing (**passes**); at least one purchase that matters is affordable but not automatic (**fails** — the pad is the item measured to matter most and a full year of a kit-buying policy spends **$235 and never reaches its $260**, while shoes sit at the other end, cheap enough to be reflexive; a wall and a reflex are both the absence of a decision); and the trade is legible while choosing (**partly** — the shop says what dead rubber costs in grades, the pad's cost in head is surfaced nowhere). Criterion 2 is the next work and it is content rather than a dial pass. **Restating a gate is not lowering it** — the new one fails today, on a measurement rather than a feeling, which is what a gate is for.
- 2026-08-21 - **The Phase 3 control was broken for five measurements** (`notes/phase3-the-control-was-broken.md`). Re-running the season probe after today's changes found something older and worse than anything today's changes did. **The `kept` control could never buy shoes.** It is supposed to be "a player for whom money is not a question" — no bills, free food, free fuel — but zeroing the bills also removed every reason to work, so it **earned nothing, ended every year on about $2, and finished on 0.89 shoe wear against a working player's 0.30.** Dead rubber is worth roughly 3.7 sends against 1.0, so that one omission accounts for the entire anomaly the first Phase 3 note recorded and could not explain: *"and **fewer** sends than the player paying rent"*. **A control that removes the costs and the income is not a control for "does money pressure climbing" — it is a control for being broke.** With a $10,000 float, so shoe wear matches at 0.29 vs 0.30, twelve seeds say: greedy **3.50 sends, 181 days, 5.51 allround, 19.5% of days worked**; kept **2.92 sends, 196 days, 5.53 allround, 0% worked** — kept wins 1, **ties 7**, loses 4. So the conclusion holds and now for a clean reason: **seventy days of not working buys fifteen climbing days and eleven burns and converts them into nothing**, landing within 0.02 of a grade of the player who worked all year. The honest reading of the sends column is *no detectable difference*. What matters more than the number: the previous five measurements reached the right conclusion **through a broken control**, and **a right answer from a wrong method is not evidence** — the paradox it produced was visible in the first note, recorded, and never chased, when an anomaly you cannot explain is the measurement telling you the instrument is wrong. For Evan's decision this changes nothing and the evidence is better: option A was already refuted, the gate still does not close on a sound control, restating it remains the live option. Two probe/game divergences fixed on the way: the probe called `NameFirstAscent` rather than `ClaimFirstAscent` — **the same half-a-verb bug the engine had**, so every simulated first ascent was worth no opinion to any faction — and `ReviewSeason` ran nowhere in either. Still divergent and recorded rather than invented: the probe has no "ask for beta" or "sit at the fire" policy, so the two things wired into the game today are not modelled, and giving the simulated player a social policy is a design choice rather than a quiet fix.
- 2026-08-21 - **Dials nobody reads, and mirrors nobody pinned** (`tools/check-dials.py`, `notes/dials.md`). The reachability sweep asked *does anything call this?*; the same question one level down lands on the project's first hard constraint. **A dial nothing reads is "dials over magic numbers" quietly inverted** — a magic number wearing a dial's clothes, advertising a tuning that does not exist. Three of 234 were dead. `AgeDials::techniquePeak` and `::headPeak`, both `999.0`, were sentinels for a code path that is *deliberately* absent — `AgeDay` does not touch technique or head and says so where it does not — and are **deleted rather than wired**, because a dial whose only value means "this code does not exist" is worse than no dial: somebody moves it and wonders why nothing happened. If head should ever decline that is a design change with a measurement, not a number to twiddle. The third, `KitDials::groundedFraction`, was not a sentinel but a **mirror** the resolver never read. That opened the second rule. Sharing a constant between two dial structs is this project's deliberate arrangement — *share the function, mirror the constant* — and it is only safe while something holds the copies together. **There are five such pairs and before today one was pinned.** Drifting would have meant: the shop quoting a price the wall does not charge, the guidebook describing a runout the resolver does not price, the physio disagreeing with the climbing, **the pad retuned at the shop with no effect at the wall** — and the one the checker found that I did not know about, **`daysPerYear` held separately by `AgeDials` and `ConditionsDials`: a birthday and a solstice on different calendars**, ages sliding against seasons across a career with no symptom sharp enough to notice until somebody turned 40 in high summer twice running. One false negative is worth keeping in view and is why the two rules must coexist: a search for `.fieldName` cannot tell `KitDials::noPadGradePenalty` from `SessionDials::noPadGradePenalty`, so the first sweep found one dead pad dial and **missed the other beside it, hiding behind its own mirror**. Mirrored names are now checked by rule 2 and excluded from rule 1.
- 2026-08-21 - **Shoes: the cheapest thing that matters, and it did nothing** (`ShoePenaltyFor`, `notes/shoes.md`). `ShoePenalty` was flagged unwired for an unusual reason — **the resolver carried its own copy of the formula**, reading from `SessionDials` while the shop read from `GearDials`. The *mirroring* is deliberate and right: it keeps `DirtbagSession` from including half the project to price a move, and two other values do the same (the runout mirrors `SportDials`, the injury penalty mirrors `BodyDials`). The rule it encodes is **share the function, mirror the constant** — and shoes were doing neither, which is exactly how `ShoePenalty` ended up with no caller. `ShoePenaltyFor(wear, edging, penalty, bite)` is now the one place dead rubber is priced, and **the golden vectors did not move**, which is what says the two copies really were identical rather than nearly so. `TestTheMirroredDialsStillAgree` now pins all three pairs, because nothing would have failed if one drifted: the shop would quote a price the wall did not charge, the guidebook would describe a runout the resolver did not price, the physio would disagree with the climbing about an injury — all silently, all visible only as "the numbers feel off". **And it was worth measuring before calling it fixed**, because until this morning's `ToSim` fix every attempt in the game resolved on brand-new shoes. Ten seasons: a climber who never replaces their rubber sends **1.0 against 3.7** on the same burns and the same days — winning 7 seeds, tying 3, **losing 0** — for about **$9** of the year's cash. That puts shoes in the category the kit measurement named as the only one that can add to a season: **kit that raises the value of skin you were going to spend anyway**, alongside the crash pad. The cheapest thing in the game that matters, and until today it did nothing at all. The gear shop now says what the rubber is costing you, because it was also the only real handicap the player had no way to see. **13 gaps down to 4**, and all four are the belayer set, held deliberately until the cave is placed.
- 2026-08-21 - **The Lot is worth something, and a checker that looks both ways** (`ShareBeta` generosity, `PsycheFrom`, symmetric `check-mirror-coverage.py`). Standing now scales what a partner spells out for you — the first thing standing has ever bought at the wall rather than on a screen — through a `generosity` argument that scales the *share* and never the *ceiling*, so a well-liked climber learns a line faster and never learns more of it than there is. Sitting at the fire lifts psyche by **the best of the people there, not the sum**: summing would make crowding the fire a strategy, and an evening is lifted by the person who lifts it, not by a headcount. That is 13 gaps down to 5. **Then the tooling hole from this morning got closed, and caught an eleventh bug on its first run.** `check-mirror-coverage.py` asked only whether `FromSim` *reads* every sim field; it now also asks whether `ToSim` *fills* every one. Three flags, not the flood I expected — and one was real: **`SessionState::shoeWear` never survived the bridge.** `StartGymSession` copies the rubber off the career into the session, and the next `ToSim(Day)` — a dozen a day — put it back to 0.0, so **every attempt in the game resolved on brand-new shoes** and the entire gear-wear economy, resoles included, cost nothing at the wall. Its old `mirror-skip` read *"Blueprint reads it from Player.Shoes.Wear"*, which was true and was the wrong question: a skipped field is not merely absent from Blueprint, it is **erased from the sim on the next call**. Worth keeping as the shape of the mistake — a justification answering *does anyone display this* when the question was *does anyone lose this*. Verified by reintroduction: deleting either `ToSim` line now fails the checker by name.
- 2026-08-21 - **Six of the thirteen wired: fuel, the salaried day, and the sponsor's side of the bargain** (**SAVE_VERSION 15**). **Fuel** is charged in `DriveVan`, which every drive in the game comes through — the way it came to be free was that each travel spot *could* have charged it and none *had* to. Charged rather than refused: you cannot decline to have burned the fuel you already burned, so a skint player arrives at the crag owing for the drive, the same way rent works, and `LastDriveFuel` carries the number to the arrival toast because a cost the player never sees reads as a bug. **The salaried day** runs from `Sleep`, immediately after `SleepToNextDay` resets the DayState to the wake hour — which is where a day begins and so where the job takes it, matching the probe exactly. At dawn rather than offered as an action, deliberately: nine to five means the clock arrives at the far side of the day having skipped everything the day was for, and **a trap you can decline is not a trap**. `SalaryOwnsHour` stays unwired and now says why — with the day worked at dawn the clock is always past the shift, so it is unreachable *by construction* rather than by omission, which is a different thing and should read as one. **The sponsor** now pays monthly and reviews yearly. The review needed `daysHurtThisSeason`, which had to become **saved state**, because being hurt pauses the review clock and a reload must not launder a season spent injured into a season spent slacking. Worth recording plainly: **`ReviewSeason` was unwired in the probe too** — every other gap was engine-only, so this one meant no career anywhere, played or simulated, had ever been reviewed, and the sponsorship changelog's "a failed review costs a rung rather than the career" was describing code that had never run outside a unit test. **And a hole in the tooling, found by trying to break the new tests:** `check-mirror-coverage.py` checks `FromSim` and not `ToSim`, so deleting the engine-to-sim half of the new field passes every checker and every test — while silently throwing the nightly increment away and stopping the injury pause from ever firing. Recorded in `notes/engine-bridge-gaps.md`, not fixed here: making it symmetric flags every derived field a `ToSim` legitimately drops, and that triage is its own pass.
